# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-18

## LLM Agents
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Learning to Steer LLM Agents and Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22316v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based agents and multi-agent systems increasingly tackle complex tasks through long-horizon tool use and inter-agent collaboration. However, these systems are prone to deviating from the overall goal as execution unfolds, which wastes execution resources and leads to undesired outcomes, a failure termed goal drift. In this work, we propose a steering agent that supervises execution on the fly and steers the system back on track when drift occurs. The steering agent observes the execution context, deciding when to intervene with a low-cost fast path and how to intervene with a high-cost slow path. To unlock its steering intelligence, the ability to assess goal drift and generate effective interventions, we jointly train the two paths with reinforcement learning, combining a pairwise ranking objective with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). Experiments on information-seeking search tasks show that the steering agent provides effective supervision for both single-agent and multi-agent systems, improving their performance and efficiency. The learned steering intelligence generalizes to out-of-domain tasks, transfers to unseen executors, and supports controllable intervention frequency without retraining.</p></details> | 21 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Librarian Agents: Training LLMs to Curate Long-Context Workspaces that Anticipate Future Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22273v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning over long document contexts remains a challenge for large language models (LLMs). Standard retrieval methods are inherently reactive, bottlenecking agents with complex, cross-document reasoning at inference time, while precomputed structures like knowledge graphs often fall short because they rely on rigid heuristics. Instead, can we allow agents to perform anticipatory directed curation: learning to proactively reorganize unstructured corpora prior to inference to efficiently serve diverse future queries. We build on a key asymmetry in modern agentic harnesses (e.g., Claude Code): while LLM agents are adept at navigating existing filesystems to solve an immediate task, they are rarely trained to construct reusable workspaces for future use. To instantiate this capability, we introduce Librarian Agents, a framework in which a builder agent transforms raw documents into a hierarchical filesystem of synthetic files and reference links, which forms a workspace tailored for a downstream answering agent. We formulate workspace construction as a Markov decision process and optimize the builder policy via reinforcement learning to directly maximize downstream answering accuracy. Empirically, Librarian Agents outperforms the strongest retrieval and precomputation baselines by 33 percentage points on BrowseComp-Plus, amortizes upfront construction costs across repeated queries, and generalizes zero-shot to unseen domains on BRIGHT with a 31-point average accuracy improvement.</p></details> |  |
| **[Harness-Bench: Evaluating Agent Harnesses across Models and Realistic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27922v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are increasingly deployed as executable systems that use tools, modify workspaces, and produce concrete artifacts. In such workflows, performance depends not only on the base model, but also on the harness: the system layer that manages context, tools, state, constraints, permissions, tracing, and recovery. However, existing benchmarks typically abstract away execution, compare complete agent systems, or hold the harness fixed, making execution-layer variation difficult to study. We introduce Harness-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark for evaluating configuration-level harness effects in realistic agent workflows. Harness-Bench evaluates representative harness configurations across multiple model backends under shared task environments, budgets, and evaluation protocols, while preserving each harness's native execution behavior. The benchmark contains 106 sandboxed offline tasks constructed from practical agent-use patterns and manually reviewed for realism, solvability, oracle-checkability, and integrity. Each run records final artifacts, execution traces, usage statistics, and validator outputs, enabling analysis beyond final completion. Across 5,194 execution trajectories, we observe substantial variation in completion, process quality, efficiency, and failure behavior across model-harness pairings. These results suggest that agent capability should be reported at the model-harness configuration level rather than attributed to the base model alone. Our analysis further identifies recurring execution-alignment failures, where plausible reasoning becomes decoupled from tool feedback, workspace state, evidence, or verifiable output contracts. Harness-Bench provides a reproducible foundation for diagnosing and improving reliable, efficient, and auditable agent execution stacks.</p></details> | <details><summary>36 pa...</summary><p>36 pages, 10 figures, 28 tables</p></details> |
| **[PROBE: Learning to Audit Policy Compliance in Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22124v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern agentic systems extend language models with external tools, enabling them to complete complex tasks through multi-turn interactions. As these agents are deployed in consequential settings, they are often constrained by policies governing pricing, privacy, confirmation, safety, and other requirements. However, existing evaluation methods leave a critical gap: capability benchmarks measure what agents can accomplish, while content-safety red-teaming measures harmful generations, but neither tests whether agents comply with predefined policies during otherwise valid tool use. Such policy violations are difficult to audit because their effects may emerge only after several turns, span multiple tool calls, or surface in downstream systems. We propose PROBE (Policy Rule Observation via Behavioral Exploration), an algorithm for training LLM-based auditors that interact with deployed agents as realistic users and probe them for policy violations through multi-turn, tool-mediated dialogue. We formalize agent auditing as a two-player Markov game and propose a composite reward that balances violation discovery, behavioral plausibility, conversation progress, and policy diversity. To study training objectives and reward design, we compare GRPO, DPO, and GFlowNet-based optimization in a controlled setting. Across extensive experiments, auditors trained with PROBE uncover substantially broader and higher-yield policy violations than same-architecture zero-shot adversaries, even when the auditor is significantly smaller than the target agent. Our results show that multi-turn behavioral auditing is a distinct and necessary axis of agent evaluation, complementing capability and safety benchmarks. PROBE offers a scalable path toward automated policy auditing for real-world agentic systems and suggests a co-evolutionary loop in which auditors expose failures and agents improve from auditor feedback.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[SoK: Trading Agents or Market Crashers? Dissecting Robustness and Security Failures in Academic Financial LLM Trading Schemes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19705v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents already trade with real capital, yet agent-security research has mostly treated them as generic agents. Trading changes the threat: a compromised agent's orders move prices that other participants act on, and an attacker who profits from a crash can trigger the shock that a non-robust agent mishandles. We present FARSIGHT (Financial Agent Robustness and Security Investigation and Global Holistic Taxonomy), a systematization that rates trading agents on robustness to market stress and on security against source manipulation, agent compromise, and agents acting as attackers. It also checks paper-level ratings against released code. Across 15 academic schemes, none earns full credit on any robustness metric, no paper describes a way to halt trading without waiting for LLM inference, and none combines source provenance with cross-source corroboration. In the code of the 7 open-source schemes, no release enforces a stop-loss or position limit outside the LLM, 4 lack a component that their paper or documentation describes, and 2 run model-written code on the host. The same gaps appear beyond finance. Restating FARSIGHT as a domain-independent threat model, we map them onto agents in healthcare, law, software, and crypto. We then trace how compromised agents spread harm, as worms and as cascades that need no further compromise. At published injection rates per hop, a channel read by ten agents lets one compromised agent start an outbreak. We derive 14 open problems and urge deployers to corroborate sources, keep interrupts that bypass the model, and enforce loss limits outside it.</p></details> | <details><summary>51 pa...</summary><p>51 pages, 15 figures, 19 tables, 206 references. SoK paper. v2 (extended version): adds a code-level audit of the 7 open-source schemes, a domain-independent threat model applied to healthcare, law, and other high-stakes domains, an analysis of cascades and agent worms, a discussion of options-trading agents, and 14 open problems</p></details> |
| **[GraphACM: Graph-based Action Consistency Measurement for Uncertainty Quantification in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22031v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Behavioral consistency across repeated executions provides an informative signal for assessing the reliability of large language model (LLM) agents. However, existing approaches typically summarize consistency with a single task-level score, without identifying where behavioral differences occur or whether actions are compared under equivalent decision contexts. We propose a graph-based framework for fine-grained behavioral consistency measurement and uncertainty quantification. Given multiple rollouts of the same task, we merge equivalent states into a directed, action-labeled graph, enabling action comparisons at shared decision states. We introduce node-level and global measures of action consistency, using the global consistency score as a proxy for uncertainty to predict task success or failure. Experiments across three LLMs on ALFWorld, AppWorld, and WebShop demonstrate that our measure is positively associated with task success and achieves an average AUROC of 0.811 for task-success prediction, outperforming the strongest behavioral-consistency baseline (0.729) and verbalized confidence (0.769). These results highlight the effectiveness of state-conditioned behavioral consistency and pinpoint where agent actions agree.</p></details> |  |
| **[Orchestrating LLM Agents for Scientific Research: A Pilot Study of Multiple Choice Question (MCQ) Generation and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18891v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Advances in large language models (LLMs) are rapidly transforming scientific work, yet empirical evidence on how these systems reshape research activities remains limited. We report a mixed-methods pilot evaluation of an AI-orchestrated research workflow in which a human researcher coordinated multiple LLM-based agents to perform data extraction, corpus construction, artifact generation, and artifact evaluation. Using the generation and assessment of multiple-choice questions (MCQs) as a testbed, we collected 1,071 SAT Math MCQs and employed LLM agents to extract questions from PDFs, retrieve and convert open textbooks into structured representations, align each MCQ with relevant textbook content, generate new MCQs under specified difficulty and cognitive levels, and evaluate both original and generated MCQs using a 24-criterion quality framework. Across all evaluations, average MCQ quality was high. However, criterion-level analysis and equivalence testing show that generated MCQs are not fully comparable to expert-vetted baseline questions. Strict similarity (24/24 criteria equivalent) was never achieved. Persistent gaps concentrated in skill\ depth, cognitive engagement, difficulty calibration, and metadata alignment, while surface-level qualities, such as {grammar fluency}, {clarity options}, {no duplicates}, were consistently strong. Beyond MCQ outcomes, the study documents a labor shift. The researcher's work moved from ``authoring items'' toward {specification, orchestration, verification}, and {governance}. Formalizing constraints, designing rubrics, building validation loops, recovering from tool failures, and auditing provenance constituted the primary activities. We discuss implications for the future of scientific work, including emerging ``AI research operations'' skills required for AI-empowered research pipelines.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Code of Theseus: Can Agents Rebuild Software from Recovered Design Documents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20068v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents increasingly design a system before implementing it, yet the data to train and evaluate them centres on implementation and testing: requirements and design artefacts are often missing, outdated or private. Source code, in contrast, is abundant. Prior work recovers architecture documentation by feeding LLMs the source code or letting agents explore it, which saturates the context, does not scale to many repositories, and yields a single artefact rather than aligned ones. We present a framework that recovers aligned stakeholder intent, requirements and architecture from a compact repository representation, a static dependency graph and key contextual files, and release the artefacts of 10,764 Java, Python, C/C++ and C# repositories as a dataset. An LLM judge rates the artefacts of three generators as clear and internally consistent, but less reliably correct and at the right level of abstraction. To test whether the artefacts carry the design, we pose a software Ship of Theseus: a coding agent rebuilds 29 Commit0 libraries from the intent alone or with the recovered requirements and architecture, given only an interface contract and part of the tests. The artefacts do not significantly change the held-out pass rate (33.7% vs. 35.8%), but bring the rebuilt module structure significantly closer to the original (module F1 from 0.54 to 0.63), with either generator and even with concise artefacts. Recovered artefacts thus convey design that tests do not capture, offering a scalable source of design-level data for agents that design before they implement, and the round trip measures this without human references.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 3 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[RSIFlow: Adaptive Self-Improvement Workflows with an Evolving Meta-Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21791v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in complex tasks, including code generation, tool use, and multi-hop question answering. Existing self-improvement methods enhance agent performance through model optimization, harness evolution, and executable artifact refinement, while recursive self-improvement (RSI) further extends optimization to the improvement mechanisms themselves. However, adapting self-improvement workflows to evolving performance bottlenecks while improving workflow orchestration through execution feedback remains a key challenge. We propose \textbf{RSIFlow}, a \textbf{R}ecursive \textbf{S}elf-\textbf{I}mprovement framework that integrates adaptive work\textbf{flow} orchestration with meta-agent capability evolution. RSIFlow diagnoses performance bottlenecks from task-agent trajectories and environmental feedback to dynamically select component-specific improvement workflows. The meta-agent then distills workflow outcomes into component-specific skills and cross-component principles, evolving its own harness to improve subsequent workflow orchestration. Experiments across six benchmarks and two backbone models show that RSIFlow achieves the SOTA average performance on all benchmarks. Ablation studies further demonstrate the contributions of adaptive workflow orchestration and meta-harness evolution. Code is available at https://github.com/1HUAN1/RSI-Flow.</p></details> |  |
| **[TLA-Verus: Generating and Solving Temporal Proof Obligations with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14923v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The TLA+ (temporal logic of actions) formal specification toolkit has seen widespread adoption in modeling modern distributed software systems including databases, storage systems, ledgers and coordination protocols. This popularity is despite its two significant limitations for software verification: Firstly, TLA+ offers no way to detect drift between software implementation and mathematical specification; secondly, TLC and TLAPS cannot provide satisfactory proofs of arbitrary temporal logic properties. We address the second limitation and take a first step towards the first by translating TLA+ specifications into Verus, an auto-active verifier built for Rust, via both a deterministic transpiler and an agentic translator. We demonstrate that our LLM-based pipeline is able to generate proof obligations from TLA+ specifications at scale, and to produce fully machine checkable proofs of both safety and liveness properties. Since temporal logic reasoning in Verus is a largely new task for LLMs, our work yields a valuable evaluation dataset, which we use to map out current generation model capabilities in Verus theorem proving.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Delegation Dilemma: Economies of Scale, Accountability, and Polycentric Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21746v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many collective-action systems are neither fully decentralized nor fully centralized: agents voluntarily organize into institutions that share organizational costs and delegate decisions to representatives. Delegation can reduce individual costs, but it also transfers control to representatives whose private objectives need not align with those of their constituents. We study this tension in a repeated game with endogenous delegation to self-interested centers. A simple reduced-form model yields a scale--accountability tradeoff: larger constituencies offer greater cost-sharing benefits to members, yet the marginal value to a center of retaining any one constituent decreases as group size grows. Consequently, the prospect of individual exit can sustain non-exploitative delegation only up to an endogenous accountable constituency size. Additionally, we identify parameter regions in which fully delegated, accountable governance, when feasible, must be polycentric. We investigate these predictions through simulations with large language model (LLM) agents. Across five matched pairs of 30-agent simulations, polycentric structures repeatedly emerge but remain fluid, exhibiting cycles of concentration, exploitation, exit, and re-formation. We further find that models of different capacity exhibit similar willingness to assume leadership but differ substantially in their exercise of authority, with Sol initiating exploitation more frequently than Luna in matched decision states.</p></details> |  |
| **[Human Interventions as Labels: Localizing, Steering, and (Sometimes) Editing the Computations Behind LLM Agent Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21708v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When an LLM agent misbehaves, a human steps in, and that intervention is usually discarded or replayed as training data. We treat it instead as a measurement: a label pointing at the internal computation that failed. We study it in a controlled agent environment, on one failure (asking a clarifying question when the request was already unambiguous), with labels from a rule-based overseer validated against blind human judgment. Claims cover eligible models, which both over-ask and sometimes act on matched unambiguous requests: five of thirteen qualified (Qwen2.5-3B, gemma-2-2b, OLMo-3-7B, Mistral-Nemo-12B, Phi-4). Four findings. First, the ask-vs-act decision is causally localizable in every eligible family: a direction learned from intervention labels flips 72-100% of held-out over-asks at a definite residual-stream depth, with site and dose chosen on training data, and random directions under the same procedure flip at most one episode. Second, over-asking has two mechanistic signatures: in four families the model demonstrably knows the request is unambiguous (the belief is decodable, causally inert and orthogonal to the decision) and asks anyway; in the fifth, the rare over-asks track different world-representations. Third, steering gated on a probe readout removes most of the simulated oversight burden while legitimate questions survive (bootstrap 95% intervals exclude zero); a prompt instruction makes it worse. Fourth, consolidating the fix into the weights yields a map, not a rule: on one model a LoRA confined to the causal band passes every criterion fixed in advance while behavior cloning on identical data breaks retention; on three families the reverse holds; on the fifth the outcome crosses the boundary across seeds. A geometric rule written down in advance to predict the regime failed on two out-of-family tests; we report the failure and stop rather than fit a replacement.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Agent Traces to Trust: A Survey of Evidence Tracing and Execution Provenance in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04990v6)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based agents are moving beyond text generation to planning, tool use, retrieval, memory access, environmental interaction, and multi-agent collaboration. Because their outputs depend on information and execution steps across an agent run, their execution processes are difficult to verify, debug, and audit. Even a correct final answer does not reveal which evidence supported its claims, whether tool calls were justified, how memory shaped decisions, or where execution failures originated. Relevant research addresses these questions across evidence attribution, tool-use safety, agent memory, and observability, but these areas emphasize different parts of the execution process. This survey brings them into a common framework through execution provenance, a typed graph of an agent run, and evidence tracing, its projection onto evidence-support relations, distinguishing evidence that supports a conclusion from execution that influences it. We introduce a six-dimensional taxonomy covering trace sources, evidence and execution units, provenance relations, tracing granularity and timing, representation forms, and trust functions, complemented by operational definitions of nine provenance relations and two cross-cutting attributes, provenance origin and trace assurance. Applying this framework to the surveyed systems and benchmarks reveals three gaps: limited integration of fine-grained provenance with multiple trust functions, no benchmark in our catalog that evaluates the complete provenance chain, and little reporting of tamper-evident or attested traces. These findings motivate research on unified trace schemas, semantic provenance, memory and multi-agent lineage, provenance-aware safety and recovery, realistic benchmarks, trace assurance, and privacy-aware auditing. Resources are available at https://github.com/xiaoqi-7/Agent-Tracing-Survey.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgentPerturb: Evaluating LLM Agent Robustness under Controlled Interaction Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21624v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world tasks are often complex and noisy, posing substantial challenges for LLM agents. Yet existing evaluations lack a systematic framework for jointly characterizing potential perturbation targets and types. We use this two-dimensional view to conduct a unified study that quantifies the robustness of current frontier models, traces the mechanisms behind perturbation-induced failures, and draws implications for robustness-oriented training. Through controlled evaluations on APEX-Agents and Toolathlon, we find that perturbations consistently degrade performance, while the dominant vulnerabilities vary across task environments. Trajectory-level analysis reveals that agents rarely recognize altered inputs and often propagate corrupted information without verification. A focused cost analysis further shows that successful perturbed trajectories can require substantially more computation. These findings motivate training objectives that cover diverse forms of noise and reward verification, uncertainty awareness, and recovery. We provide a rigorous benchmark-construction pipeline. Code and data will be released at https://github.com/xuyj233/AgentPerturb.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 10 figures. Project repository: https://github.com/xuyj233/AgentPerturb</p></details> |
| **[SecureClaw: Clawing Back Control of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09549v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents face two distinct security failures: unauthorized external actions and exposure of sensitive plaintext inside the runtime before any final output check can intervene. Existing defenses usually protect one boundary, either the planner/runtime or the point where external actions are executed, and therefore do not by themselves secure both surfaces. We present SecureClaw, a dual-boundary architecture that enforces authorization in a trusted executor and keeps raw protected data outside the LLM runtime. Sensitive reads pass through a trusted gateway that replaces raw values with reference IDs. In the evaluated deployment, the gateway also provides short planning summaries that deliberately release selected information, a process called declassification. Writes that change external state follow a PREVIEW$\rightarrow$COMMIT protocol in which only a trusted executor may execute the exact request authorized by policy. The runtime can still plan over summaries and reference IDs, but cannot directly resolve the IDs into secrets or perform side effects. Across the AgentDojo, AgentLeak, and Agent Security Bench (ASB) benchmarks, SecureClaw is the only defense we evaluate in a common harness that simultaneously retains usable task utility and achieves 0% attack success rate (ASR) on ASB, 0.64% ASR on AgentDojo, and 3.23% overall leak on AgentLeak's attacked parity lane, which measures final-output and internal-relay leakage. Code is available at https://github.com/ponyyuhan/SecureClaw.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster); 29 pages including appendices. This version corrects the conference footer only; scientific content is unchanged</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking the Role of Tool Graph Memory in Multi-Turn Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21571v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents solve complex tasks by calling external tools over multiple turns. Recent work has proposed tool graph memory, which stores past experience as tool transitions to guide subsequent selection. However, it remains unclear whether this component consistently improves agents' tool use or undermines it. We evaluate tool graph memory on nine models and two benchmarks, finding that it seldom improves significantly over baselines, and that its gains, where present, cost over twice the LLM calls. The memory's guidance is often incorrect, and random or no guidance retains most of the gain, implying that the benefit stems from extra computation rather than the memory itself. Moreover, even with the correct next tool suggested, many failures remain, such as giving up without a tool call. Storing the same experience instead as textual insights outperforms tool graph memory with almost no extra LLM calls. This suggests that memory serves better as a soft prior than as a prescription of the next tool, leading to a more effective and generalizable solution.</p></details> |  |
| **[Independent Data-Integrity Verification for LLM-Driven Functional Coverage Closure in Hardware Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21570v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents can write directed tests that close functional coverage, but coverage only shows that a state was reached, not that the data on the way there was correct. This is a long-known point in verification and we do not claim it as new. We describe a small coverage-closure agent that applies it to an LLM loop: coverage is read from the report that the simulation itself exports (cocotb-coverage YAML) and never taken from the model's account, and data integrity is checked by scoreboards that are separate from the model. On three architecturally distinct RTL designs (an asynchronous FIFO, a UART transmitter and an AHB2APB bridge) the agent raised coverage from 91.7%, 88.9% and 67.4% to 100% within its 8-iteration budget in every logged run, using one design-agnostic agent. A reactive scoreboard monitor attached to the FIFO's base testbench also found a clock-domain-crossing bug in the author's own stimulus code. The monitor is not yet attached to the LLM-generated tests, so their data integrity is not independently checked; one manual check on a single FIFO test is reported as preliminary. We list the weaknesses of this evidence: three designs, no baseline, no ablation, and logged runs that come from several agent checkpoints.</p></details> |  |
| **[$Φ$-Bench: Can Large Language Models Engineer the Infrastructure That Powers Them?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10226v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) now show strong reasoning and coding abilities, raising the prospect that they could assist in developing and optimizing the very infrastructure that powers them. Existing benchmarks, however, mainly focus on isolated kernels, predefined operators, or pre-specified optimization targets, and cannot evaluate open-ended, long-horizon LLM infrastructure engineering. To address this gap, we present $Φ$-Bench, a benchmark of 85 tasks grounded in real LLM training and inference codebases. The tasks cover nine categories of LLM infrastructure and come in three formats, ranging from kernel function completion to long-horizon implementation and repository-wide end-to-end optimization. They are built by a construction pipeline that turns real pull requests, issues, and repository code into executable tasks with validated tests, and every reward is gated by correctness and anti-hacking checks. Across eight frontier models, the strongest model, Claude Opus 5, scores only 36.53%, and every model performs worse on long-horizon, repository-level implementation than on kernel-level tasks. Trajectory analysis further suggests that stronger models treat optimization as controlled experimentation, validating ideas with cheap local runs and ruling out confounders before drawing conclusions. We will open-source $Φ$-Bench to facilitate future research on LLM agents' ability to develop and optimize the LLM infrastructure that powers themselves.</p></details> |  |
| **[ContinualSkillBench: Can LLM Agents Truly Evolve Their Capabilities?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03874v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern agent frameworks equip large language models with external skill libraries to solve complex tasks. However, it remains unclear whether these systems can effectively evolve their skills and whether the resulting skills improve task-solving capabilities. To bridge this gap, we introduce ContinualSkillBench, a dynamic evaluation framework for in-context continual skill learning. It covers five domains, each with 100 tasks ordered by difficulty and opportunities for skill transfer. Our experiments show that sequential execution generally improves performance, but the gains vary substantially across models and domains. Moreover, across two models and three domains, retaining context and feedback without skill maintenance yields nearly the same mean performance as maintaining an explicit skill library. Explicit skills nevertheless provide selective benefits for tasks requiring reusable procedures or precise outputs. We further find that the weaker model (GPT-4o) accumulates more skills but reuses them less often, suggesting difficulty consolidating task-specific experience into reusable procedures. These findings show that current in-context skill evolution mechanisms can support continual adaptation, but still struggle to consistently consolidate experience into robust and transferable skills.</p></details> |  |
| **[RT-J: Large-Scale Pretraining of Relational Transformers for Context-Efficient Predictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17867v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models for tables and relational databases are increasingly used for predictive tasks across various scientific and industrial domains. However, adapting existing models to unseen datasets requires tens of thousands of examples. Here we introduce RT-J, an 86M-parameter relational foundation model (RFM) that makes accurate predictions even with hundreds of in-context examples or fewer. To achieve this, we first assemble the Join, a large collection of 639 web-crawled databases with 6,023 LLM-generated forecasting tasks. We pretrain with long contexts of up to 8,192 database cells, using in-context examples from a novel lightweight random-walk retriever. Finally, we leverage context tuning and ensembling to scale up test-time compute. On in-context regression tasks from RelBench, RT-J requires 10x fewer examples on average to match strong feature engineering pipelines with LLM-agent or RDBLearn featurizers and TabICLv2 or LightGBM predictors. Overall, our work pushes the frontier for context-efficient predictions on relational data and highlights the importance of real-world semantics, context engineering and large-scale pretraining for RFMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026. Website: https://star-project.stanford.edu/rt-j</p></details> |
| **[JevAgentHarness: Benchmarking, Deploying and Training Decision Models That Share Control with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21412v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents now fix software, operate computers and serve customers, but their harnesses, the programs that run them, call a large LLM at every step, which makes long tasks slow and costly. Most of these calls end in a multiple-choice decision, such as which tool to call next. Decision models such as Jev answer these questions directly, at a fraction of an LLM call's time and price. We ask how a harness can delegate these decisions to a decision model with little loss of success. To evaluate decision models in agentic tasks, we build AgentDecisionBench, which tests them from isolated questions up to control of a running agent, on public benchmarks and our DecisionGym environment. The decision model most accurate on isolated questions solved only 69.5% of agent tasks, and without a fitted confidence threshold, delegating tool choices lowered success by 19.0 points. Building on these findings, we then design JevAgentHarness, which fits this threshold for each choice on the agent's own trajectories and lets the LLM decide below it. Because existing decision models never see the states an agent reaches after its own mistakes, we train AgentDecision-4B, an open 4B model, on such states with Jev's answers as labels. On tasks unlike its training data, the agent solved 91.5% of them with AgentDecision-4B and 82.0% with Jev. On tasks like their training data, 4B models trained this way made agents 81.1-89.0% cheaper and up to 2.5 times faster, without losing 3 or more points of task success. Across the recorded training configurations, which use different optimization budgets, it consumes 55.6% fewer training tokens than SkillOpt. Gains concentrate in particular calculations alongside regressions on others, and paired accuracy intervals include zero. CausalEvo turns task feedback into controlled decisions about skill retention and replacement. Project repository: https://github.com/txbrook/CausalEvo</p></details> | <details><summary>GenAI...</summary><p>GenAI for Health Workshop @ NeurIPS 2026, Sydney</p></details> |

To bridge this gap, we propose Agent-BOM, a unified structural representation for agent security auditing. Agent-BOM models an agentic system as a hierarchical attributed directed graph that separates static capability bases, such as models, tools, and long-term memory, from dynamic runtime semantic states, such as goals, reasoning trajectories, and actions. These layers are connected through semantic edges and security attributes, transforming fragmented execution traces into queryable audit paths. Building on Agent-BOM, we develop a graph-query-based paradigm for path-level risk assessment and instantiate it with the OWASP Agentic Top 10. We further implement an auditing plugin in the OpenClaw environment to construct Agent-BOM from live executions. Evaluation on representative real-world agentic attack scenarios shows that Agent-BOM can reconstruct stealthy attack chains, including cross-session memory poisoning and tool misuse, capability supply-chain hijacking and unexpected code execution, multi-agent ecosystem hijacking, and privilege and trust abuse. These results demonstrate that Agent-BOM provides a unified and auditable foundation for root-cause analysis and security adjudication in complex agentic ecosystems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Redundant or Necessary? A Benchmark for Detecting Redundant Steps in Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29893v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computation cost is a critical challenge for LLM agent systems, as their long-horizon trajectories continuously accumulate computational overhead. In this paper, we propose and formulate a new research problem: \textbf{redundant step detection}, aiming to detect these steps that consume substantial computational resources while contributing little or nothing to task completion. Our experiments reveal that the redundant step detection is still challenging even for SOTA models, such as Claude-Opus-5, particularly without prior information. | **[Do LLMs Learn from Rewards in Context? : Rethinking the role of reward in In-Context RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11152v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents increasingly improve at inference time by accumulating experience in context rather than by updating parameters. Trajectories drive improvement, but not through their semantic content: shuffled or corrupted trajectories work as well as real ones. These patterns closely mirror those known in ICL, suggesting that direct ICRL is better understood as a special case of ICL than as inference-time RL. Across three model sizes on BFCL, starting near completion is more effective under a fixed turn-level threshold rule. Finer handoffs alone do not consistently help, but outcome-informed tool-call selection outperforms GRPO on trained and untrained task categories. Guided starts also improve AppWorld success on both evaluation splits. These findings highlight where learning starts and how starting positions move as key curriculum choices.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 4 figures, 4 tables. Minchan Kim and Sungho Bae contributed equally</p></details> |
| **[Can LLM Agents Efficiently Identify Appliance Faults? Insights From Diagnosing a Simulated Appliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19291v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In so-called "Industry 4.0," integrated sensors, analytics tools, and other digital technologies supplement industrial workflows, potentiating enhanced visibility and automation. Prognostics and health management (PHM) and natural language processing (NLP) practitioners alike have explored the application of large language models (LLMs) to industrial asset management, but many prior works' focus on exam-like benchmarks has limited their assessment of LLMs' ability to automate practical operations. The LLMs we evaluate struggle to consistently identify faults from the sensor information alone, but the best-performing ones can iteratively refine the fault-option space to eventually obtain a successful diagnosis. Our subsequent analyses, therefore, focus on: differentiating models by diagnostic efficiency (RQ1); examining the lagging performance of the three worst models (RQ2); and identifying viable heuristics for selective prediction (RQ3), including that initial diagnostic uncertainty and LLM-judged soundness of reasoning are high-signal predictors of diagnostic success. In addition to our benchmark results (Section 3.1), we provide practical insights for improving the performance of the open-weight models on CoffeeBench, such as by explicitly tracking episode state on their behalf to reduce timeouts (Section 3.2) or applying a deliberation loop to refine diagnostic hypotheses (Section 3.3).</p></details> |  |
| **[When Is Enough Not Enough? Illusory Completion in Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07549v3)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In agentic search, an LLM agent searches the web, reads the pages it finds, and decides what to look for next before returning an answer. We call this illusory completion. To see how it arises, we introduce the Epistemic Ledger, which tracks at every turn what the retrieved pages establish about each constraint and what the agent claims. | **[Narrowing the Collaboration Gap, Probably](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19215v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are increasingly deployed as teams of agents that hold different private information about a shared task. We first analyze a simple synthetic setting that captures the structure of multi-round reasoning under partial information. We then test the same principle in a collaborative maze-solving task with trained transformers and pretrained LLM agents. Our results demonstrate that probability communication outperforms action communication when the exchanged predictions preserve calibrated uncertainty. When communicated probabilities are biased, we show that the benefits of probability communication can disappear. We give a post-hoc conversational calibration intervention that consistently improves decision-making by correcting these biases both in a toy transformer setting and when pretrained LLMs use the corrections in context. These results suggest that probabilistic communication is useful for multi-agent collaboration not merely because it transmits more information, but because calibrated uncertainty gives other agents a reliable object to update on.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgentCIScope: Grounded Contextual Integrity Evaluation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19158v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are becoming increasingly capable of handling daily workspace tasks by interacting with application environments through tool calls. In this work, we introduce AgentCIScope, a unified platform and benchmark for evaluating agent-level contextual integrity through executable, multi-step tasks across diverse, realistic application environments. AgentCIScope transforms public and synthetic privacy cases into executable agent tasks, with disclosure constraints grounded in three criteria: governing privacy policies, persona-based agreement, and a predefined sensitive-attribute taxonomy. Based on AgentCIScope, we evaluate 12 agents on 936 tasks and find that strong task-completion capability does not guarantee contextual-integrity preservation: single-run leakage rates range from 32.5% to 94.4%, while repeating each task five times further increases leakage by 4-19 percentage points. Leakage varies substantially across attributes: personal preferences and booking details have average leakage rates of 84.7% and 76.5%, compared with 17.1% for credit-card information and 29.4% for SSNs; environments: calendar has the highest average leakage rate as an information source, while WhatsApp ranks highest as an information destination; and governing privacy policies. | **[Plan or Retrieve First? A Scale Study of Tool Discovery Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19068v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-augmented LLM agents discover relevant APIs from large registries before planning execution, yet finite context windows prohibit injecting all tool definitions into a single prompt. The dominant approach, which we term Retrieve-then-Plan (RtP), embeds the user query and retrieves similar tools via approximate nearest-neighbor search. We study an alternative Plan-then-Retrieve (PtR) paradigm in which the LLM first generates Abstract Tool Stubs (hypothesized function signatures describing needed capabilities) which then serve as retrieval queries against the registry. Evaluating on ToolBench (46,984 unique API endpoints, 50 categories) across single-tool (G1), intra-category multi-tool (G2), and cross-category multi-tool (G3) query subsets, we find a clear complexity-dependent crossover: RtP outperforms PtR on single-tool queries while PtR outperforms RtP on multi-tool queries. This crossover holds at every retrieval budget (k=1 to 20), is robust across registry sizes from 1k to 47k APIs, and is consistent across model families (Gemini 2.5 Flash and gpt-oss-120b). We characterize a stub-hallucination failure mode (the LLM invents plausible but non-existent API names) that acts as a double-edged sword: on simple queries PtR's stubs abstract away proper-noun signals that RtP matches directly, yet on complex queries the same mechanism decomposes multi-faceted intent into functional sub-needs. We further analyze token overhead and show that PtR's extra discovery call roughly doubles inference cost, which pays off on multi-tool queries but hurts on single-tool ones, motivating complexity-adaptive routing as a practical direction, one that can be driven by a zero-cost signal already present in the pipeline (RtP's top-1 retrieval score).</p></details> |  |
| **[Automated Standardization of Legacy Biomedical Metadata Using an Ontology-Constrained LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08552v4)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Descriptive scientific metadata in public repositories are often incomplete and inconsistent with community standards and ontologies, limiting data FAIRness. Entry of metadata standards through prompt engineering offers only fixed snapshots of the relevant standards, however, and it relies on an LLM's pretrained knowledge to interpret and satisfy constraints related to the standard. We evaluate whether giving an LLM access to metadata specifications and authoritative terminology at runtime improves automated metadata standardization. We present ARMS, a tool-augmented LLM agent that retrieves complete CEDAR metadata templates and dynamically queries authoritative biomedical terminology services at execution time. We compared ARMS with a prompt-based approach on 839 legacy metadata records from the Human BioMolecular Atlas Program (HuBMAP), using expert-standardized records as the reference standard. ARMS outperformed the prompt-based approach, increasing precision from 0.56 to 0.93 and recall from 0.51 to 0.85, with improvements across all field categories and assay types. The largest gains occurred for ontology-constrained fields, where precision increased from 0.36 to 0.92. Our findings show that access to appropriate metadata standards can substantially improve LLM-based standardization of legacy metadata. ARMS improves metadata standardization by giving LLMs runtime access to the authoritative resources that define valid metadata. Representation of metadata standards in machine-actionable form enhances the task of rectifying legacy metadata using LLM technology, especially when those standards can be queried dynamically.</p></details> |  |
| **[CyberArena: Benchmarking AI Agents in Live Symmetric Attack-and-Defense Combat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19005v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are demonstrating increasingly strong offensive and defensive cybersecurity capabilities in the real world. Current benchmarks mostly evaluate agents in isolation against static targets. Real cyber operations, however, are dynamic and coupled: security postures constantly change as attackers and defenders adapt to each other in real time. Static benchmarks quickly saturate and miss an agent's ability to attack and defend simultaneously under live pressure. To address this gap, we present CyberArena, a dynamic, symmetric adversarial benchmark built around multi-round attack-and-defense combat. In isolated sandboxes, two agents compete under identical rules, acting concurrently as both attackers and defenders over continuously changing services. This converts static targets into evolving, semi-open challenges, directly testing an agent's speed and precision in discovering, exploiting, and patching vulnerabilities. To sustain the benchmark's longevity, an automated agentic pipeline continuously generates, reviews, and validates new environments. Furthermore, because pairwise contests are notoriously hard to quantify, we introduce a dynamical model that maps match dynamics into unified, multidimensional scores across offensive, defensive, and domain-specific capabilities. Evaluating 12 frontier LLM agents across 40 challenges in 466 matches, CyberArena separates model capabilities where single-agent baselines hit a ceiling. The trajectories read like a fight rather than an exam: defenders patch one hole and miss the next and certify repairs on stale evidence, while capable agents think a move ahead, planting backdoors for later rounds and, in one case, writing their own packet sniffer to watch the opponent. Beyond a benchmark, CyberArena serves as an adaptable, scalable experimental platform for future research on cybersecurity agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[ChunkAct: Action Chunking for Agentic Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18874v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic robotics places a large language model (LLM) agent in the robot control loop, where it composes perception, motion, and vision-language-action (VLA) tools under the guidance of a memory and thereby generalizes to new tasks without retraining. This generality comes at a steep price in efficiency. Every primitive requires a round of LLM reasoning over the full interaction history, so the cost of an episode grows with the number of primitives. We find this reasoning largely redundant: once a sub-goal is chosen, its primitives follow a routine already prescribed by memory. We present ChunkAct, which introduces action chunking to agentic robotics. Online, a single call launches a chunk of chained primitives, which executes autonomously and returns control only upon failure. Offline, ChunkAct compiles the memory into these chunks, so execution is memory-free at test time and the planner is reserved for decisions that require reasoning. LLM computation therefore scales with the number of decisions rather than the number of primitives. On LIBERO-Pro, ChunkAct improves task success by 5.0 points over the state of the art in agentic robotics while requiring 2.9x fewer LLM calls, 4.4x fewer input tokens, and 2.3x fewer output tokens.</p></details> |  |
| **[Hybrid Reasoning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18861v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How can we build an agent that thinks beyond human language, yet still acts through it in the external environment? Architecturally, a lightweight HybridLink enables seamless transitions between text and latent modes, while the agent's continuous working memory preserves lossless hybrid context across multiple turns. These designs preserve tool-integrated agentic capabilities while making latent thoughts directly inspectable. Building on this architecture, we further develop dedicated post-training algorithms for hybrid reasoning: HybridSFT stabilizes tool-integrated hybrid generation by teaching the backbone to coordinate with HybridLink, then HybridRL scales reasoning by learning when to think in latent space and when to act through text. Compared with frontier LLM Agents and agentic frameworks spanning both text-based and latent methods, HybridAgent consistently improves performance by 10.4%, while delivering 35.5%-54.7% token reduction, 1.8x-2.6x faster inference, and 2.7x-3.7x cost savings. | **[ProAS: A Progress-Aware Advantage Shaping Framework for Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18683v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as the primary paradigm for optimizing Large Language Model (LLM) agents, yet efficient critic-free algorithms (e.g., GRPO) suffer from a fundamental uniform credit assignment dilemma that broadcasts trajectory-level advantages equally to all actions. To resolve this without introducing critic overhead, we propose Progress-Aware Advantage Shaping (ProAS), a plug-and-play framework that leverages a dynamically evolving Agentic Reference Graph (ARG) to achieve fine-grained credit assignment via Masking Unreasonable Incentives (MUI) and Asymmetric Penalty Redistribution (APR). Extensive experiments across Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct demonstrate that ProAS universally elevates mainstream group-based baselines to new state-of-the-art performance, achieving peak success rates of 91.9\% and 94.3\% on ALFWorld, alongside 66.7\% and 70.8\% on WebShop, respectively. Furthermore, it accelerates training convergence by up to $2.44\times$ while maintaining strong robustness against imperfect or distilled ARGs. ## Medical Large Language Models
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[TriageRA-CCF: Source-Side Clinical Confidence and Coverage Signals for Adaptive Rank Budgeting in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29375v2)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models are commonly adapted with a fixed low-rank budget, even though medical questions differ substantially in confidence, clinical coverage, and cross-domain difficulty. We study adaptive rank budgeting for parameter-efficient medical question answering: for each question, the adapter decides whether to activate a small, medium, or large subset of LoRA rank channels. The central challenge is that a naive adaptive budget router can collapse to unstable choices or spend capacity without improving shifted benchmarks. We propose TriageRA-CCF, a source-side teacher for adaptive rank-budgeted LoRA. It combines three signals computed only from source training data: base-model answer confidence, metadata-cell clinical coverage, and a counterfactual close-miss proxy. These signals supervise a straight-through budget router over active ranks {2,4,8}, together with budget-cost, entropy, and rank-balance regularization. Under a matched CMB-source training protocol, TriageRA-CCF achieves the best average accuracy among LoRA, DoRA, and MoELoRA baselines on both Qwen3-8B and Llama3.1-8B. The gains are modest and non-uniform across benchmarks: +0.21 average points over the strongest external baseline on Qwen3-8B and +0.16 on Llama3.1-8B. Component ablations show that confidence, coverage, and counterfactual signals all provide useful budget supervision, but their combination is not monotonically best on every backbone.</p></details> |  |
| **[MedRouter: Demystifying Knowledge Differences Across Medical LLMs for Routing-Based Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33119v1)** | 2026-09-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical question answering spans diverse specialties and modalities, and individual medical large language models (LLMs) exhibit distinct strengths across tasks and domains. This heterogeneity suggests that combining specialists may enable broader coverage of medical questions than relying on any single model. However, existing LLM routing methods primarily seek to balance answer quality and inference cost, leaving open how to exploit differences in specialist competence to improve medical reasoning. In this paper, we introduce MedRouter, an agentic system that uses an embedding-based multi-label router to select and query specialist LLMs, then passes their responses to a generator to produce the final answer. We further propose SCALE (Specialist Competence-Aware Learning), a two-stage training framework that first trains the Router with specialist correctness supervision and then optimizes its selections through reinforcement learning. The second stage uses a Performance Gain Reward (PGR) that measures how specialist information affects the generator's answer correctness relative to answering without that information. Experiments on eight text-based and multimodal medical QA benchmarks show that MedRouter outperforms the strongest routing baseline by 8% in average accuracy. Our analysis of specialist outputs further reveals distinct strengths and complementary question-level coverage, motivating learned routing to combine these capabilities for more comprehensive medical reasoning.</p></details> |  |
| **[HiMed: Incentivizing Hindi Reasoning in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24635v2)** | 2026-09-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models hold promise for reducing healthcare disparities, yet Hindi remains severely underrepresented. While medical LLMs excel in high-resource languages, their performance degrades sharply in Hindi, particularly on Indian systems of medicine. We argue that robust cross-lingual medical transfer requires Hindi reasoning. To this end, we introduce HiMed, a Hindi reasoning medical corpus and benchmark suite covering both Western and Indian medicine. We further propose HiMed-8B, a Hindi-form medical reasoning LLM, through the design of decaying scaffolding reward. Extensive experiments demonstrate improvement in Hindi medical reasoning performance and a reduction in the English-Hindi accuracy gap. Ablation studies validate the contribution of each training stage and reward component. All code, data, and weights are available</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[A General Multimodal Probing Framework for Data Fusion and Prediction with Frozen Large Language Models: Evidence from Multimodal EHR Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28798v2)** | 2026-09-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The main objective of this paper is to propose a general framework for prediction based on different sources of multimodal data in the healthcare domain. We evaluated whether frozen medical large language model (LLM) representations can serve as a shared embedding space for multimodal primary diagnosis category prediction. We propose a backbone-flexible probing framework that serializes structured electronic health record (EHR) variables and leakage-pruned discharge-note sections into a shared frozen-LLM representation space and recovers primary diagnosis categories with linear probes, without fine-tuning the LLM. On a MIMIC-IV cohort of 13,645 admissions (seven diagnosis categories), we probed inputs of different modalities (Structured-only, Unstructured-only, and Combined) across transformer layers and six frozen backbones, compared against XGBoost and three text baselines (CAML, LAAT, PLM-ICD) under a matched split and context budget, and tested robustness across datasets on MIMIC-III. The Combined terminal-layer probe reached 88.86% top-1 accuracy on MIMIC-IV, significantly outperforming CAML and PLM-ICD and statistically comparable to LAAT; Combined accuracy ranged from 87.69% to 89.25% across six backbones. On MIMIC-III, in-domain Combined probing reached 74.18% accuracy and significantly exceeded all three baselines, and in a transfer-learning test a 2.8M-parameter adapter on frozen MIMIC-IV representations reached 92.51% accuracy, exceeding adapted LAAT (85.54%). Frozen LLM embeddings unify structured and narrative EHR information: the Combined configuration was strongest in every setting, and its representations transferred across datasets through a compact adapter. Multimodal probing of frozen LLM representations provides a practical, backbone-flexible approach for studying EHR modalities and adapting clinical representations across datasets.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages (14 main text + 16 supplementary), 3 figures, 4 tables; supplementary materials appended after the references</p></details> |
| **[The widening evaluation gap in medical large language model research 2023 to 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11770v1)** | 2026-09-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are superseded every few quarters; clinical evidence takes years. We asked whether medical research is keeping pace with the systems it evaluates. PubMed returned 11,628 records for January 2023 to June 2026 across fourteen clinical domains, growing 45-fold; 2.5% used a randomised, controlled or prospective design. Evaluation lag, from a study's newest named model release to its own publication, widened from 1.33 to 6.08 quarters. Because discontinued models age mechanically, we benchmarked this against a counterfactual holding model composition fixed: migration to newer systems offset only 56% of the drift (95% CI 50-65). Randomised trials evaluated models a median 4.6 quarters older than other designs (P = 3 x 10^-19), yet among studies naming a model still under development no design differed from any other; 62% of randomised trials evaluated a discontinued family. Rigour and currency are in tension, and that tension reflects model selection rather than research timelines.</p></details> |  |
| **[VeriSim: A Configurable Framework for Stress-Testing Medical AI Under Patient Communication Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10441v2)** | 2026-09-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models are typically evaluated on idealized patient cases that do not reflect how real patients communicate. Truth adherence is supported by a verifier that extracts atomic claims from each candidate utterance and judges them against a UMLS-grounded vector index built with BioLORD embeddings, using the retrieved atoms' structured clinical metadata (e.g., drug class, anatomical site, treats-condition relations) rather than surface-text similarity alone. Across seven open-weight LLMs, realistic noise reduces diagnostic accuracy by 15-25 percentage points and increases conversation length by 34-55%; the 7-8B models degrade 1.4x more than 70B+ models. A board-certified physician and a licensed nurse rate VeriSim's conversations highly on truth, realism, clinical utility, and noise fidelity (inter-annotator agreement >= 0.80 across all dimensions), and an LLM-as-judge closely tracks their ratings, enabling scalable evaluation. We release VeriSim as an open-source framework for rigorous stress-testing of clinical AI: https://github.com/mohitmarvania/VeriSim</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 9 figures, 13 tables. Accepted to Findings of EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[Benchmarking Clinical Decision Pathway Adherence in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26592v1)** | 2026-08-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Following clinical decision pathways (CDPs) defined by clinical practice guidelines is essential for safe and reliable medical decision-making. Experiments on 16 representative LLMs show that reliable clinical decision support remains challenging for current models, demonstrating the need for CDP-oriented evaluation and the value of MEGA-CDP for advancing guideline adherence in medical LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Didactic knowledge or Clinical Cases? We address this issue with token-matched experiments that vary the didactic-to-clinical ratio and analyze how data composition affects performance, capability profiles, and error patterns across knowledge-intensive and clinic-oriented tasks. We uncover an asymmetric transfer across task types: clinical data improves clinic-oriented tasks while remaining competitive on knowledge-intensive ones, whereas didactic data mainly improves knowledge-intensive tasks. Error analysis suggests a knowing-doing gap, where improvements in knowledge recall do not reliably generalize to clinical reasoning. We further observe that modest amounts of clinical data yield most of the gains on EHR-grounded tasks, while the optimal mixture ratio varies with the knowledge and clinical reasoning demands of downstream tasks. These findings suggest that medical LLM data curation should be application-driven, with higher proportions of clinical data preferred for reasoning-intensive use cases.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This paper is accepted by NLPCC 2026 oral</p></details> |

The final experiment uses 300 clinical cases and produces 8,100 model responses across three prompting conditions. We report persona level accuracy, counterfactual consistency, correct consistency, and narrative sensitivity error. Qwen2.5 7B achieves the best accuracy at 56.33 percent and the best correct consistency at 40.33 percent. | **[Checkup2Action: A Multimodal Clinical Check-up Report Dataset for Patient-Oriented Action Card Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11533v4)** | 2026-08-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Routine clinical check-up reports combine laboratory measurements, physiological assessments, imaging findings and visually structured information, but rarely tell patients what to do next. | **[CardioBench: A Real-World Data Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models in Clinically Authentic Cardiovascular Care Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25186v2)** | 2026-08-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Background: Most medical large language model (LLM) benchmarks focus on examination knowledge or isolated tasks and may not reflect the longitudinal, multimodal, and safety-critical workflow of cardiovascular care.
