| **[Discovering state-of-the-art quantum circuits with large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22367v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Significant efforts are currently being directed toward the implementation of large-scale quantum information processors, but realizing useful quantum advantage also requires the development of efficient quantum algorithms. While for many practical problems the dominant resource costs are associated with a small set of computational primitives, optimizing them in the quantum setting presents unique challenges, including a reversibility constraint absent from ordinary classical computation. Here we introduce an evolutionary framework in which large language models (LLMs) propose circuit designs and an independent evaluator verifies and scores them. We apply this framework to tasks spanning numerical computation, chemistry, optimization, quantum simulation, and number theory. In seven of the eight tasks, the discovered circuits significantly reduce both Toffoli and qubit counts relative to the state of the art. In the eighth, the search discovers a new reversible architecture for prime-field modular inversion. By handling reversibility differently, the architecture makes divsteps -- a classical algorithm previously impractical for reversible integer arithmetic -- viable and substantially improves the Toffoli-qubit trade-off for modular inversion. These results open the door for evaluator-guided LLM co-design of new algorithmic structures for quantum computation.</p></details> | <details><summary>46 pa...</summary><p>46 pages, 10 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[Grounding Generative Verification in 3D Content](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22360v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and 3D generation have expanded 3D understanding and synthesis. However, beyond visual fidelity, task-specific correctness grounded in 3D evidence remains a major challenge. We argue that building reliable 3D systems requires not only stronger generation and reasoning, but also verification feedback that identifies unmet requirements and explains their violations, allowing systems to reflect on and refine their outputs. Such feedback must integrate multi-view observations and distinguish genuine inconsistencies from projection-dependent appearances. We make three contributions. First, we introduce 3DVerifierBench, a benchmark spanning 10 tasks that evaluates decisions, rejection explanations, and responses to controlled changes in specifications and candidates. A human study reveals a 21.6 percentage-point gap between humans and the strongest tested model, GPT-6 Astra, while explanation-aware analysis exposes weaknesses in rejection reliability. Second, we analyze verification across fine-grained factual categories, exposing errors in values and target binding. We develop a scalable pipeline combining multi-view fact extraction, controlled 3D editing, and native annotations to produce 14,126 training examples with contradiction explanations and linked counterfactuals. Third, we examine how verification could support 3D generation through candidate selection and recursive refinement. In human-labeled sixteen-candidate pools, oracle selection improves complete factual success from 26.7% to 90.0%. In a separate eight-input study, human-guided recursive refinement satisfies all seven requirements for every input, compared with three under parallel Oracle@8. Together, our benchmark, analysis, and data pipeline establish a foundation for models to reflect on 3D content through reliable verification feedback.</p></details> | <details><summary>35 pa...</summary><p>35 pages, 6 figures. Project page: https://ai-yeslab.github.io/3DVerifier-page/</p></details> |
| **[MLExpert: Towards a Generalist Agentic Model for Autonomous Machine Learning Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22344v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are expanding from assisting scientific research and engineering to autonomously developing and improving AI systems, advancing AI for AI (AI4AI) as a pathway toward recursive self-improvement (RSI). Workflow-driven machine learning engineering (MLE) systems provide a practical path toward this pursuit through complex orchestration that manages long-horizon experimentation, while recent MLE systems adopt model-directed interaction, allowing models to autonomously organize experiments and adapt optimization strategies based on execution feedback. However, feedback from each experiment reflects several preceding decisions, and a long MLE run contains many such costly experiments. Training must therefore select useful supervision from long interaction trajectories and learn from intermediate outcomes. We propose a post-training method for autonomous long-horizon MLE optimization and apply it to train MLExpert, a 35B Mixture-of-Experts agentic model. We design the Kaggle-MLE Factory to construct executable tasks and an interaction harness for model-directed experimentation. Hierarchical agentic SFT transfers general tool-use and coding capabilities to MLE through outcome-guided supervision. Windowed Interaction RL (WIRL) treats the multi-turn decisions between successive experiment results as an execution window and assigns it a shared outcome-based reward. Asynchronous collection and policy correction support policy updates before the trajectory ends. On MLE-Bench Lite, MLExpert improves the average medal rate of the base model from 37.9% to 72.7%, achieving performance comparable to larger frontier models such as Kimi-K3 and GLM-5.3. Further experiments show that MLExpert improves performance on NatureBench Lite, agent scaffold optimization, and algorithm optimization, suggesting that the learned optimization capability generalizes to other tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/InternScience/MLExpert Model: https://huggingface.co/InternScience/MLExpert</p></details> |
| **[Evaluation Awareness Beyond English: Do LLMs Act Differently When Tested in Indic Languages?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22333v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can often tell when they are being tested. This ability, known as evaluation awareness, can lead models to act more cautiously during safety evaluations than in real use. Existing work has studied evaluation awareness almost entirely in English, although models serve users in many languages. Indic languages are spoken by over a billion people, yet evaluation awareness remains almost entirely unstudied in them. We test four instruction-tuned models from three families in five Indic languages alongside English. A set of cues varies whether the model is told that it is monitored and whether the monitoring is a safety test. Models refuse harmful requests more often when they are told that they are being tested. This increase appears in all four models and all six languages. In Indic languages, refusal of harmful requests rises by 14.4 percentage points on average. The test cue also brings Indic refusal of harmful requests level with English, but raises refusal of benign Indic requests from 6.9% to 11.3%. We release all 47,880 judged responses to support further work. These results suggest that safety evaluations in Indic languages may not reflect how models treat their everyday users.</p></details> |  |
| **[Designing Transformers for Continuous Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22330v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Continuous diffusion language models (CDLMs) offer an alternative approach to parallel language generation; however, most existing studies directly use transformer architectures from either causal large language models (LLMs) or Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) for image generation, which were not optimized for CDLMs. In this paper, we conduct a systematic study on Transformer architectures for CDLMs and introduce Language Diffusion Transformer (LangDiT), combining feature and query-key normalization, wider noise conditioning, likelihood-shortcut gradient routing and calibration, and confidence-gated training self-conditioning. LangDiT significantly reduces generation perplexity in matched LangFlow comparisons, establishing a new state of the art in continuous language generation across different model formulations. Using LangFlow as a representative CDLM, LangDiT as a plug-and-play backbone leads to a new state-of-the-art model with a generation perplexity (GenPPL) of 21.72 using 1024 NFEs and 44.72 GenPPL using 128 NFEs on the unconditional generation of 1024-token sequences on the OpenWebText dataset. When adapted to Plaid-style CDLM formulations, LangDiT results in a new leading GenPPL of 11.33 using 1024 denoising NFEs, and 27.51 GenPPL using 128 denoising NFEs. These gains are achieved while retaining each model's underlying representation, training objective, and inference sampler, isolating transformer architecture as a fundamental source of improvement. Our study identifies architectural design as an important source of improvement in continuous diffusion language models.</p></details> |  |
| **[Training Reasoning Models to Generate Diverse Scientific Ideas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22324v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>One of the first steps of a research pipeline is generating ideas. Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used to automate this step, but most of this work optimizes the quality of individual ideas, while overlooking diversity. In our work, we study document-grounded ideation, focusing on increasing the diversity of LLM generated ideas and ensuring they are high quality. Given prior papers, a model must propose a set of ideas that are each high-quality and clearly distinct from one another, while also being grounded in the input documents. To optimize for this, we propose training with a set-level reinforcement learning objective. In our setup, in a single generation, the model writes K structured idea cards grounded in the source papers, and the set is scored jointly, where each idea's quality is weighted by its distance from the ideas written before it. This teaches the model to steer each new idea away from its predecessors. We show that our trained 8B model achieves higher creative utility (a measure combining quality and diversity) than Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-6 Astra on our contamination-free benchmark. In a pilot human study with four experts, our sets are judged as promising as Claude Opus 4.8's and contain marginally more unique ideas. When a coding agent runs proof-of-concept experiments on the ideas, our model's success@k is comparable to Claude Opus 4.8's, so we see that the added diversity does not cost any executability.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code and Data at:https://github.com/ManyaWadhwa/diverge_ideation | Under Review</p></details> |
| **[Learning to Steer LLM Agents and Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22316v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based agents and multi-agent systems increasingly tackle complex tasks through long-horizon tool use and inter-agent collaboration. However, these systems are prone to deviating from the overall goal as execution unfolds, which wastes execution resources and leads to undesired outcomes, a failure termed goal drift. In this work, we propose a steering agent that supervises execution on the fly and steers the system back on track when drift occurs. The steering agent observes the execution context, deciding when to intervene with a low-cost fast path and how to intervene with a high-cost slow path. To unlock its steering intelligence, the ability to assess goal drift and generate effective interventions, we jointly train the two paths with reinforcement learning, combining a pairwise ranking objective with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). Experiments on information-seeking search tasks show that the steering agent provides effective supervision for both single-agent and multi-agent systems, improving their performance and efficiency. The learned steering intelligence generalizes to out-of-domain tasks, transfers to unseen executors, and supports controllable intervention frequency without retraining.</p></details> | 21 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Grid-Bench: Can LLMs Predict How Discrete Worlds Change?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22312v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial instructions describe how to move objects, transform regions, or modify their surroundings. When several operations are applied in sequence, each acts on a configuration shaped by earlier changes. How accurately can large language models (LLMs) predict the resulting spatial state? We introduce Grid-Bench, an executable benchmark in which eight families of operations transform discrete worlds represented as two-, three-, and four-dimensional grids. Given an initial grid, explicit rules, and an event history expressed as text, a model predicts the complete final grid. Reference execution makes every predicted value checkable, supporting comparisons across spatial operations, history lengths, and workloads. Evaluating six LLMs shows that complete-state accuracy falls sharply as histories grow, with substantial divergence among models that perform similarly on single events. Models with similar exact-match accuracy can nevertheless differ substantially in the number of incorrect cells on shared instances. Even over long histories, some models recover many correct changes beyond what copying the initial state would yield. Grid-Bench makes both complete recovery and these differences in returned states directly measurable across a range of spatial conditions.</p></details> | <details><summary>39 pa...</summary><p>39 pages, 15 figures, 15 tables, including appendices. Project website: https://grid-bench.github.io/ ; Code: https://github.com/grid-bench/Grid-Bench ; Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/xzx34/Grid-Bench</p></details> |
| **[AI Consilium: a Benchmark of LLM Clinical Multidisciplinary Second Opinions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22298v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can generate differential diagnoses for complex cases, but a useful second opinion must also be factually grounded, clinically coherent, actionable, and safe. Eight single-model pipelines generated independent role-specific opinions and interactive-consilium reports under free-form and structured clinical-reasoning conditions. Structured final reports had higher overall mean scores in seven of eight model pipelines, with their clearest advantage in management (2.740 vs. 2.436). Compared with the structured pre-consilium primary-role opinion, the complete structured synthesis had higher pooled scores across all five shared criteria, with the largest increases in problem prioritization, factual grounding, and reasoning coherence. GPT-5.6-Sol led every evaluated component and reached consensus in the fewest review rounds. These findings show that the benchmark can distinguish model capability, the contribution of multidisciplinary synthesis, criterion-specific effects of response structure, deliberation dynamics, and inference requirements within one common-case evaluation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Private Retrieval Augmented Generation via Random Projection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22297v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances the capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by querying external structured knowledge. By applying the same projection to both datastore embeddings and query embeddings, our method provably preserves semantic similarity between queries and retrieved items while substantially mitigating data extraction attacks. Across multiple RAG architectures and datasets, we show that this lightweight approach achieves superior retrieval and generation performance compared to prior methods which leverage formal differential privacy (DP) guarantees, while exhibiting comparable empirical privacy under strong attack models. Our results for the first time suggest that random projection can serve as a competitive and practical baseline for privacy-preserving RAG systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Continual Learning: Mitigating Forgetting With Subspace Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22293v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) acquire new skills through fine-tuning, but it can destroy capabilities they already have: catastrophic forgetting remains a central obstacle to continual learning in LLMs. Recent works connect forgetting to how far, and along which directions, fine-tuning moves the weights. On-policy methods such as reinforcement learning and on-policy distillation avoid updating the principal singular directions of weight matrices and thus forget less. In contrast, SFT concentrates updates along these directions and is susceptible to catastrophic forgetting. In this work, we introduce Subspace Tuning (SST), which restricts each weight update to the bottom singular subspace, eliminating changes along the principal directions. SST is optimizer-agnostic and composes well with fine-tuning recipes including SFT and self-distillation. SST combines tight subspace protection, dense gradient estimation, and a simple refresh mechanism, enabling stronger learning than prior orthogonal fine-tuning methods. With these mechanisms, protecting as much as 99\% of the weight subspace substantially improves retention while preserving strong target-task performance. This behavior strengthens with model and data size and nearly eliminates forgetting in the large-model regime. SST also keeps the model close to its base distribution, inducing $2$--$4\times$ less KL drift than the baseline recipes. Overall, SST advances the learning--forgetting Pareto frontier, making it a practical default for fine-tuning pre-trained language models.</p></details> |  |
| **[CAT-alyzing Honesty: Continuous Adversarial Training for Transferable LLM Honesty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22292v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are becoming prevalent in scenarios where honesty is critical. However, despite alignment training, these models still exhibit dishonest behaviour, such as outputting claims that contradict some knowledge or are not supported by what is accessible in context. CATCH trains the model towards an honest answer and away from a dishonest one while maintaining the model's capabilities. We evaluate two architectures (Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct and Qwen3-3.6-27B). We show that trained with only hundreds of targets built from self-reporting scenarios, CATCH learns an honest behaviour that robustly generalizes and transfers across distinct benchmarks. It consistently reduces dishonesty under pressure on MASK, increases confessions in the multi-agent game of Among Us, and improves the discovery rates of the model's hidden behaviours in AuditBench. We show that CATCH outperforms prompting and supervised fine-tuning in improving honesty, and it is on par with state-of-the-art honesty activation steering. However, CATCH surpasses honesty steering in maintaining utility, thanks to a novel choice of utility data.</p></details> | 9 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Agentic-MatriBO: Capability-Compiled Agentic Bayesian Optimization for Materials and Chemical Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17832v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Materials and chemical optimization must choose the next synthesis, characterization, or high-fidelity simulation from sequential observations under a limited budget. We present Agentic-MatriBO, a capability-compiled agentic Bayesian optimization (BO) framework that represents BO components and selection conditions as composable capabilities. A language policy proposes BO recipes and candidate plans; a compiler checks proposals against the registered capability catalog; and a deterministic execution layer validates and commits candidates. Oracle observations are stored in research memory and inform later rounds. We compare Agentic-MatriBO with eight baselines on four offline finite candidate pools---COF, PCE10, Buchwald--Hartwig (BH), and Suzuki--Miyaura---using five seeds per condition; results are reported descriptively. Across the four tasks, Agentic-MatriBO has the best mean endpoint quality after orienting each objective so that higher is better. On COF, it reaches the tabulated optimum in all five seeds both in the end-to-end comparison and after shared initialization, where it also retains an advantage in cumulative quality. Its normalized area under the best-so-far quality curve during the BO stage (nAUC-BO) is substantially higher on COF, near parity with the best baseline on PCE10 and BH, and lower on Suzuki. COF ablations show that removing the capability compiler or evidence gate coincides with lower cumulative quality and fewer exact-optimum hits. The framework provides an inspectable interface for evidence-conditioned BO adaptation.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, 9 figures, supplementary information included</p></details> |
| **[Librarian Agents: Training LLMs to Curate Long-Context Workspaces that Anticipate Future Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22273v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning over long document contexts remains a challenge for large language models (LLMs). Standard retrieval methods are inherently reactive, bottlenecking agents with complex, cross-document reasoning at inference time, while precomputed structures like knowledge graphs often fall short because they rely on rigid heuristics. Instead, can we allow agents to perform anticipatory directed curation: learning to proactively reorganize unstructured corpora prior to inference to efficiently serve diverse future queries. We build on a key asymmetry in modern agentic harnesses (e.g., Claude Code): while LLM agents are adept at navigating existing filesystems to solve an immediate task, they are rarely trained to construct reusable workspaces for future use. To instantiate this capability, we introduce Librarian Agents, a framework in which a builder agent transforms raw documents into a hierarchical filesystem of synthetic files and reference links, which forms a workspace tailored for a downstream answering agent. We formulate workspace construction as a Markov decision process and optimize the builder policy via reinforcement learning to directly maximize downstream answering accuracy. Empirically, Librarian Agents outperforms the strongest retrieval and precomputation baselines by 33 percentage points on BrowseComp-Plus, amortizes upfront construction costs across repeated queries, and generalizes zero-shot to unseen domains on BRIGHT with a 31-point average accuracy improvement.</p></details> |  |

In practice, however, the final text may be mixed-source, with watermark evidence surviving at only a subset of token positions after rewriting, insertion, deletion, or paraphrasing. Although prior work has studied global detection of watermark signals, when such signals can be localized remains unclear. We formulate watermark localization as a token-level multiple-testing problem based on pivotal statistics, with a latent indicator recording whether watermark dependence survives at each position. Under an asymptotic regime indexed by exponents for signal sparsity, next-token concentration, and effective-vocabulary growth, we derive a sharp boundary for global detection and phase transitions for discovery and classification within the class of coordinatewise pivot-based localization rules. We show that discovery is strictly harder than detection and that consistent classification is impossible across the parameter regime within this class. We then develop an adaptive thresholding method that does not require knowledge of the exponents or time-varying next-token distributions, but uses a data-driven estimate of the surviving watermark fraction. The method attains the optimal discovery boundary and near-optimal discovery power relative to homogeneous pivot-based rules. Updated experiments and supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[Baselines and Score Centering in Policy Gradient Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22263v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning has become a widely used approach to post-training large language models. In a typical implementation, learning is off-policy: The policy generating the responses differs from the policy being trained because of training-inference mismatch or policy staleness in asynchronous training. This discrepancy is commonly addressed by importance sampling, whose ratio is usually regularized in practice through truncation, masking, or clipping. Under regularized importance sampling, the importance-weighted policy gradient is no longer invariant to constant shifts in the reward. This invariance is recovered by using the covariance between the reward and the importance-weighted score as the estimand, which coincides with the policy gradient when the ratio is not regularized. This note develops this covariance perspective for both sequence-level and token-level importance sampling. The covariance perspective reveals the roles of the group-mean baseline and score centering in policy gradient estimation. Both perform centering in covariance estimation, but they differ in the mean being subtracted: The group-mean baseline uses a sample mean, whereas score centering uses a population mean. The two lead to distinct unbiased covariance estimators with different variances. Whether both forms of centering should be used simultaneously depends on whether importance sampling is applied at the sequence level or at the token level. With sequence-level importance sampling, score centering adds no benefit once the group-mean baseline is used; with token-level importance sampling, using the group-mean baseline without score centering generally introduces bias when the importance ratio is regularized.</p></details> | 17 pages |
| **[BitSparse: Compact Activation Storage for ReLU Feed-Forward Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22256v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reducing memory use in large language model (LLM) training is an ongoing challenge. Activation checkpointing saves memory by recomputation, while lossy compression changes the stored activations. The reemergence of sparse $\relu$ and $\relu^2$ activations creates a third opportunity: losslessly compressing the activation cache itself. We present BitSparse, a GPU-resident lossless compression format for sparse activations. Bfloat16 (BF16) and FP8 activations are supported. Optional sign-bit packing further increases compression up to close to the theoretical limit. We analyze its compression ratio and compare it to the theoretical optimum. BitSparse lowers per-token VRAM by 31\% and 30\% Modded-NanoGPT and Nemotron-H respectively, with overheads of no more than 1\% at practical sequence lengths. Additionally, a new preallocated buffer system avoids host synchronization during training when variable size tensors are allocated.</p></details> |  |
| **[WaveLLM: Wavelet-Domain LLM Entropy Modeling for Lossless Image Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22232v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lossless image compression fundamentally relies on accurate entropy modeling, driving a recent shift toward large language model (LLM)-based methods. However, existing approaches mainly emphasize the modeling capacity of LLMs while directly serializing images into pixel or residual sequences, leaving heterogeneous signal patterns entangled within a single prediction stream. In this paper, we argue that effective entropy modeling depends not only on model capacity, but also on the predictability of the input representation. Motivated by this insight, we propose WaveLLM, a wavelet-domain LLM-based entropy model for lossless image compression. By decomposing an image into multi-level wavelet subbands, WaveLLM converts a heterogeneous prediction problem into a set of more structured and statistically homogeneous subproblems that are more amenable to autoregressive probability estimation. To bridge numerical coding symbols with the LLM's native text token space, we introduce a dedicated numerical interface featuring value embeddings and a coefficient prediction head. Furthermore, a task-adaptive prompting strategy is designed to enable a single shared model to adapt to diverse subbands within a unified framework. Built on Llama-3.2-3B with LoRA adaptation, WaveLLM achieves 2.51, 2.24, and 2.27 bpsp on Kodak, CLIC.pro, and DIV2K, respectively, outperforming the PixelCNN-based codec using the same wavelet transform by 0.43, 0.36, and 0.46 bpsp, and surpassing recent LLM-based lossless compressors on all three benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at ACM Multimedia 2026 (MM '26). 10 pages, 6 figures</p></details> |
| **[Language Models Struggle to Use Representations Learned In-Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04212v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Though large language models (LLMs) have enabled great success across a wide variety of tasks, they still appear to fall short of one of the loftier goals of artificial intelligence research: creating an artificial system that can adapt its behavior to radically new contexts upon deployment. One important step towards this goal is to create systems that can induce rich representations of data that are seen in-context, and then flexibly deploy these representations to accomplish goals. Recently, Park et al. (2024) demonstrated that current LLMs are indeed capable of inducing such representation from context (i.e., in-context representation learning). The present study investigates whether LLMs can use these representations to complete simple downstream tasks. We first assess whether open-weights LLMs can use in-context representations for next-token prediction, and then probe models using a novel task, adaptive world modeling. Code is available at https://github.com/ponyyuhan/SecureClaw.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster); 29 pages including appendices. This version corrects the conference footer only; scientific content is unchanged</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking the Role of Tool Graph Memory in Multi-Turn Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21571v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents solve complex tasks by calling external tools over multiple turns. Recent work has proposed tool graph memory, which stores past experience as tool transitions to guide subsequent selection. However, it remains unclear whether this component consistently improves agents' tool use or undermines it. We evaluate tool graph memory on nine models and two benchmarks, finding that it seldom improves significantly over baselines, and that its gains, where present, cost over twice the LLM calls. The memory's guidance is often incorrect, and random or no guidance retains most of the gain, implying that the benefit stems from extra computation rather than the memory itself. Moreover, even with the correct next tool suggested, many failures remain, such as giving up without a tool call. Storing the same experience instead as textual insights outperforms tool graph memory with almost no extra LLM calls. This suggests that memory serves better as a soft prior than as a prescription of the next tool, leading to a more effective and generalizable solution.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Perception to Prediction and Intervention: Benchmarking Proactive Video Assistants in Real-Life Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21568v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent multimodal large language models have advanced video understanding, but existing benchmarks mainly evaluate whether models can interpret observed visual content. It evaluates three complementary capabilities: daily perception, risk prediction and intervention. We benchmark 12 representative open-source and proprietary video-language models, including streaming and adapted non-streaming systems. Our evaluation shows that current models struggle to make well-calibrated response decisions. Finally, we construct additional proactive training data and train \textsc{P$^3$I-Assistant-4B}, achieving improvements on several tasks, though gains remain uneven across capabilities. | **[BiShield-TEE: On the (In-)Security of Unilateral Weight Obfuscation in On-Device TEE-Shielded LLM Partition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21565v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>On-device deployment of large language models (LLMs) is increasingly adopted due to latency and privacy requirements, but exposes proprietary models to reverse engineering and intellectual property theft. Trusted Execution Environments (TEEs) provide hardware-enforced isolation for on-device inference, yet most TEE-based parameter obfuscation relies on unilateral matrix transformations that obfuscate only output side. We identify a vulnerability in this design, termed the \emph{Solution Determinacy Property (SDP)}: the obfuscation secret is uniquely determined, exposing the structure of the true key and enabling attackers to break the protection. Under our attacks, representative methods \textsc{GroupCover} and \textsc{ArrowCloak} are reduced to negligible protection, with attack accuracy reaching over $2.4\times$ the full-shielding black-box baseline and recovering near-complete model functionality.To address this vulnerability, we propose \textsc{BiShield-TEE}, a bilateral framework that induces \emph{Ambiguous Solutions} through joint obfuscation of input and output dimensions, hiding the true key among many equally valid candidates. Experiments across diverse models and datasets show that \textsc{BiShield-TEE} confines attack accuracy to the full-shielding black-box baseline, while achieving higher inference efficiency than prior methods on real hardware.Our code is available at https://github.com/laduiw/BiShield-TEE.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. We release DialFork under the MIT license at https://github.com/idiap/dialfork, hoping the community will measure conversational process alongside content.</p></details> |  |
| **[Can Crowdsourcing Survive the LLM Era? A Community Survey on Human Data Collection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04924v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The widespread use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as writing tools challenges the validity of crowdsourced data, as crowdworkers may outsource tasks to models. While 93% of them had anticipated this, half were unsure what precautions to take. The most prevalent detection strategies are distinctive textual style patterns and unusually fast completion times. | **[Mind the Shift: Decoding Monetary Policy Stance from FOMC Statements with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14313v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) statements are a major source of monetary-policy information, and even subtle changes in their wording can move global financial markets. Rather than relying on manual hawkish--dovish labels, DCS uses consecutive meetings as a source of self-supervision. It learns an absolute stance score for each statement and a relative shift score between consecutive statements. A delta-consistency objective encourages changes in absolute scores to align with the relative shifts. This allows DCS to recover a temporally coherent stance trajectory without manual labels. Across four LLM backbones, DCS consistently outperforms supervised probes and LLM-as-judge baselines, achieving up to 71.1% accuracy on sentence-level hawkish--dovish classification. Variance partitioning reveals that SCRIBE explains unique brain-predictive information beyond syntactic features, suggesting that its representations capture aspects of linguistic meaning not accounted for by the measured syntactic features. Together, these findings establish SCRIBE as a scalable and interpretable foundation for studying natural language semantics in the brain.</p></details> |  |
| **[Decomposing Brain-LLM Alignment with Interpretable Semantic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21508v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) predict neural responses during naturalistic language comprehension, but the linguistic information underlying this brain alignment remains difficult to interpret. | **[ISW-Agent: Integrating LLM-Driven Dialogue with n8n Workflow for Internal Solitary Wave Remote Sensing Image Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21502v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Internal solitary wave (ISW) remote sensing requires task parameters and spatial information to be transferred across image acquisition, detection, stripe extraction, and propagation prediction. | **[$Φ$-Bench: Can Large Language Models Engineer the Infrastructure That Powers Them?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10226v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) now show strong reasoning and coding abilities, raising the prospect that they could assist in developing and optimizing the very infrastructure that powers them. To address this gap, we present $Φ$-Bench, a benchmark of 85 tasks grounded in real LLM training and inference codebases. The tasks cover nine categories of LLM infrastructure and come in three formats, ranging from kernel function completion to long-horizon implementation and repository-wide end-to-end optimization. They are built by a construction pipeline that turns real pull requests, issues, and repository code into executable tasks with validated tests, and every reward is gated by correctness and anti-hacking checks. Across eight frontier models, the strongest model, Claude Opus 5, scores only 36.53%, and every model performs worse on long-horizon, repository-level implementation than on kernel-level tasks. We will open-source $Φ$-Bench to facilitate future research on LLM agents' ability to develop and optimize the LLM infrastructure that powers themselves.</p></details> |  |
| **[On the Privacy of LLMs: An Ablation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02255v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) face diverse privacy threats, yet existing studies typically evaluate individual attacks in isolation, offering limited guidance on which system factors drive privacy vulnerabilities. This paper presents a unified threat model and a systematic ablation study of four representative attacks: Membership Inference (MIA), Attribute Inference (AIA), Data Extraction (DEA), and Backdoor Attacks (BA). Our findings reveal attack-specific sensitivities: membership inference depends strongly on dataset characteristics and attack strategy, attribute inference is influenced by model and training characteristics, data extraction increases with model scale and training-data repetition, and backdoor attacks achieve consistently high success rates. Based on these findings, we derive practical recommendations for privacy-aware LLM deployment, including prioritizing membership leakage assessments in retrieval-augmented systems, minimizing repeated personal information in training data, monitoring attribute inference risks, and safeguarding fine-tuning pipelines against backdoors.</p></details> |  |
| **[Reasoning Error from Known Fact: Step-Level Self-Consistency Group Relative Policy Optimization for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18915v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs), modern systems not only possess strong foundational capabilities and extensive knowledge, but can also solve complex problems via long, multi-step reasoning. To address this issue, we propose Step-level Self-Consistency Group Relative Policy Optimization (SSC-GRPO), which assigns step-level rewards to reasoning traces by computing self-consistency scores of individual steps across multiple rollouts. Compared with prior methods, SSC-GRPO achieves state-of-the-art performance on both mathematical reasoning benchmarks and hallucination leaderboards. Our results offer a new perspective for detecting and mitigating hallucinations in the reasoning process of large language models.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Reliable Long-Context Reasoning: Detecting Unanswerable Questions via FactGuard](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05607v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite the success of Large Language Models (LLMs) in long-context tasks, their reliability in recognizing unanswerable questions remains a critical challenge. By training with FACTGUARD-BENCH, we improve overall accuracy to 81.17% and significantly enhance models' reasoned rejection capabilities. Our work provides a scalable path toward building more faithful and trustworthy long-context LLMs. Our source is publicly available at https://github.com/biaoliu-kiritsugu/FactGuard.</p></details> |  |

## Medical reasoning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Taking the PULSE of Medical Reasoning: Step-Level Retrieval Timing for Knowledge-Grounded Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20292v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable medical reasoning requires LLMs to dynamically access external biomedical evidence to improve factual trustworthiness and reasoning accuracy. However, indiscriminate retrieval can introduce noise and latency, raising a critical retrieval-timing problem: at each reasoning step, the system must decide whether the model's internal knowledge is reliable or external evidence is needed. We propose PUlSE, a lightweight probing method that uses the model's internal representations as intrinsic signals for step-level retrieval control. Our posterior trajectory analysis reveals that erroneous steps exhibit substantially higher inter-layer volatility than correct ones, suggesting unstable internal knowledge. Based on this finding, PUlSE trains a compact probe on inter-layer geometric features to detect factual errors upon step completion, and uses it as an adaptive trigger for retrieval and step regeneration. Across four medical benchmarks, PUlSE-timed retrieval matches exhaustive per-step retrieval in accuracy while reducing retrieval calls by 72%, and can transfer to an unseen legal domain, indicating potential domain-agnostic generality. Overall, our study provides an initial step toward shifting retrieval-trigger design from surface-level confidence estimates to internal activation dynamics for adaptive access.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning When to Trust Medical Knowledge: Regression-Safe Routing for Clinical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19663v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical reasoning LLMs need external medical knowledge, but retrieved evidence and reasoning chains can be outdated, poisoned, or hallucinated. Harmful knowledge can cause regression: changing a correct answer into an incorrect one. We construct a medical knowledge-poisoning benchmark covering retrieved evidence and reasoning chains, with seven attack families filtered by a three-judge "subtle-but-wrong" vote and paired clean exams that separate poisoning effects from question difficulty. No fixed knowledge-use strategy is robust across these settings: strong RAG methods trade robustness to poisoned evidence against accuracy when evidence is useful. We formulate knowledge use as per-instance routing and introduce Med-TrustRouter, a lightweight controller that keeps the solver frozen. From leakage-free runtime signals, it estimates each action's expected correctness gain and uncertainty, preserving the base answer unless another action is confidently better. Across seven medical reasoning settings, Med-TrustRouter achieves 78.9 macro and 69.2 worst-case accuracy, matching the strongest competitor on macro accuracy while substantially improving the worst-case floor. Its gains transfer across solvers and evidence judges, unlike a hand-coded router using the same signals. Similar performance across linear, tree, and neural action-value heads suggests that the benefit comes from routing rather than estimator complexity. These results support learning when to trust external knowledge for robust clinical reasoning.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 8 figures, including supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[From Given to Gathered Evidence: Agentic Learning for Longitudinal Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39566v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models can serve as clinical agents through tool-use harnesses. However, conventional medical benchmarks assess reasoning over preselected evidence rather than the ability to seek it across clinical records and longitudinal imaging. We propose CASE: a series of role-specific Clinical Agents for Seeking Evidence, together with a tool-use harness and an agentic post-training framework for compact vision-language policy models. We further introduce a longitudinal multimodal benchmark built on UK Biobank, comprising 50,401 clinical questions derived from real-world ICD-10-coded diagnoses of 4,739 participants. Each question links to a patient-specific environment containing clinical context and multi-sequence MRI from baseline and follow-up visits, where agents autonomously select which visits, organs, modalities, slices, and specialist tools to inspect and compare. Supervised fine-tuning transfers evidence-seeking workflows from 147,434 frontier-model interaction trajectories, followed by agentic reinforcement learning on the learner's own environment interactions. Privileged on-policy self-distillation and rubric-based LLM feedback refine evidence-to-conclusion reasoning without prescribing tool sequences. Experiments show that CASE moves beyond question-answer imitation toward transferable investigation policies, strengthening evidence-grounded longitudinal reasoning. Under matched evaluation conditions, our Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct based agent achieves over 16% and 10% relative improvements in answer accuracy over GPT-5.4 and Claude Opus 4.8. Project page available at: https://vinyehshaw.github.io/case-project/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/VinyehShaw/CASE ; Hugging Face: https://huggingface.co/CASE-Bench</p></details> |
| **[MedEvo: Medical Reasoning Evolution through Knowledge Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17690v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical multimodal reasoning models are typically trained on teacher-generated rationales, discarding the valid reasoning in students' own failed trajectories, while general-domain correction methods offer little guidance for errors in image reading, anatomical context, or differential diagnosis. We propose MedEvo, which uses medical knowledge, namely a tag taxonomy and error-type diagnosis, to navigate both trajectory repair and data selection. For each failed rollout, a gold-blind auditor locates the first consequential error and provides a minimal, answer-free hint; the same student retains the valid prefix and regenerates the erroneous suffix. After each round, MedEvo identifies capability gaps across medical tags on a held-out feedback set and prioritizes related questions for new rollouts and repairs. With supervised fine-tuning (SFT) alone, MedEvo raises Qwen3.5-9B mean accuracy across seven benchmarks from 65.57% to 69.12% (47.40% to 54.50% on the multimodal track of MedXpertQA (MedXpertQA-MM)), surpassing teacher-distillation SFT (66.93%). It also raises Qwen3.5-35B and Qwen3.5-397B mean accuracy from 67.91% to 73.04% and from 71.83% to 74.95%, respectively. Our analyses show that failed trajectories contain reasoning worth preserving, that repair success does not imply learning value, and that independent feedback is key to sustained improvement.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project: https://pork-pay.github.io/MedEvo/; GitHub: https://github.com/pork-pay/MedEvo</p></details> |
| **[MedPRMBench: A Fine-grained Benchmark for Process Reward Models in Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17282v2)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical reasoning can reach a plausible answer while overlooking an unsafe or unsupported intermediate step. Process-Level Reward Models (PRMs) can verify such steps, yet existing PRM benchmarks largely target mathematics rather than patient-specific clinical errors. We introduce \textbf{MedPRMBench}, a step-level medical reasoning benchmark with 14 clinical error types, including five new and two clinically re-scoped types, and rule-defined severity grades. Built from seven medical QA sources via structured error construction and review by 86 physicians from Grade-A tertiary hospitals, it provides 6{,}500 evaluation questions, 13{,}000 reasoning chains, and 113{,}910 step labels, plus a leakage-clean 6{,}870-question training split. The best of 20 prompted critics reaches only 75.4\% PRMScore, and critics differ sharply in error-type profiles and operating points. A Qwen3-8B PRM trained on the split reaches 86.6\% PRMScore and 89.0\% strict first-error localization; in matched ablations, physician review raises error recall by 6.55 points and cuts clean-chain false alarms from 5.6\% to 2.1\%. The training signal replicates across model families; without further training, the PRM transfers to ProcessBench and PRMBench, exceeding six of nine math-trained PRMs on each.</p></details> |  |
| **[Lingdan-V2: Large language models for clinically grounded reasoning in traditional Chinese medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14705v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models have advanced medical reasoning, but clinically grounded reasoning in traditional Chinese medicine (TCM) remains challenging because prescription decisions require integrating domain knowledge, syndrome differentiation, treatment principles, herbal prescription composition, and dosage determination. We develop a TCM language model family comprising models with 4 billion, 8 billion, and 14 billion parameters. The artificial intelligence contribution is a staged clinical alignment framework integrating large scale continued pretraining on TCM and clinical records, complementary reasoning supervision, and prescription-centered reinforcement learning (RL). We further formulate herbal prescription generation as a structured RL problem with prescription-level feedback, enabling direct optimization of clinical decision outputs beyond knowledge reproduction and generic instruction following. The engineering application is TCM clinical decision support, particularly herbal prescription recommendation. The 14 billion parameter reinforcement aligned model achieves a prescription generation F1 of 37.0 on the public benchmark, compared with 32.3 for DeepSeek-R1 with 671 billion total parameters, and a herb-level F1 of 52.8 on the clinical dataset with improved dosage agreement. Reinforcement post-training consistently improves prescription recommendation across all scales, while physician-source analysis shows that expert heterogeneity affects reward stability. These findings demonstrate a scalable pathway from domain grounding and reasoning supervision to clinically oriented prescription decision support.</p></details> |  |
| **[MedMemoryBench: Benchmarking Agent Memory in Personalized Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11814v2)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The large-scale deployment of personalized healthcare agents demands memory mechanisms that are exceptionally precise, safe, and capable of long-term clinical tracking. However, existing benchmarks primarily focus on daily open-domain conversations, failing to capture the high-stakes complexity of real-world medical applications. Motivated by the stringent production requirements of an industry-leading health management agent serving tens of millions of active users, we introduce MedMemoryBench. We develop a human-agent collaborative pipeline to synthesize highly realistic, long-horizon medical trajectories based on clinically grounded, synthetic patient archetypes. This process yields a massive, expertly validated dataset comprising approximately 2,000 sessions and 16,000 interaction turns. Crucially, MedMemoryBench departs from traditional static evaluations by pioneering an "evaluate-while-constructing" streaming assessment protocol, which precisely mirrors dynamic memory accumulation in production environments. Furthermore, we formalize and systematically investigate the critical phenomenon of memory saturation, where sustained information influx actively degrades retrieval and reasoning robustness. Comprehensive benchmarking reveals severe bottlenecks in mainstream architectures, particularly concerning complex medical reasoning and noise resilience. By exposing these fundamental flaws, MedMemoryBench establishes a vital foundation for developing robust, production-ready medical agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[Frozen Models, Evolving Expertise: Model-Agnostic Learning from Deployment Experience for Multimodal Medical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09146v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) are usually frozen after deployment, so they do not learn from the cases they solve. This is especially concerning in medicine, where new clinical evidence, updated guidelines, and new therapies can change established practice. Fine-tuning can update the model, but it requires access to model weights and additional training. Parameter-free methods avoid training, but they may overfit a fixed validation set, lack reliable domain knowledge, or lose visual details by saving experience only as text. To address these limitations, we present a model-agnostic framework that allows frozen LLMs and VLMs to learn from deployment experience through three forms of external expertise: a Skill that guides reasoning and tool use, a Knowledge Memory that stores reliable facts supported by earlier cases or trusted external evidence, and a Multimodal Knowledge Base that keeps visual examples and guides the model to relate each retrieved case to the current image. Instead of relying on a fixed validation set, a validation strategy keeps an update only if it helps on new cases without degrading performance on earlier ones. Across six benchmarks covering clinical diagnosis, clinical workflows, medical reasoning, and medical and non-medical visual reasoning, and with four open-weight and closed-source base models, our framework improves performance during online deployment by up to 34.2% over the base model on medical tasks, generalizes to unseen cases, transfers to other models without further optimization, and works in non-medical domains.</p></details> |  |
| **[MedZERO: Self-Evolving Agents for Open-Ended Medical Reasoning Through Controlled Knowledge Accumulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08327v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in medical question answering and clinical reasoning, yet their improvement remains constrained by static parametric knowledge and costly expert supervision. We present MedZERO, a self-evolving framework for open-ended medical reasoning. MedZERO couples an Examiner that generates frontier medical question-option pairs with a Reasoner that solves them through evidence-grounded multi-turn reasoning with external knowledge tools. To support reliable, continual improvement, MedZERO adopts controlled knowledge accumulation, which maintains temporary exploratory knowledge and curated persistent knowledge in reasoning. We evaluate MedZERO on five public medical reasoning benchmarks using 4B- and 8B-scale base models under open-ended evaluation. Across all settings, MedZERO consistently outperforms the underlying base models and prior self-evolving baselines, achieving up to 13.7 average accuracy-point gains over the next-best self-evolving baseline.</p></details> | <details><summary>accep...</summary><p>accepted by NIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[FedCoT: Communication-Efficient Federated Reasoning Enhancement for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10020v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enhancing LLM reasoning in federated settings is nontrivial due to stringent computational, communication, and privacy constraints, especially in healthcare, where clinically consequential decisions require not only accuracy but also interpretable, auditable rationales to meet safety, accountability, and regulatory requirements. Experiments on medical reasoning benchmarks show consistent gains under tight resource budgets while keeping data local and respecting privacy, offering an interpretable and resource-efficient solution. Our code is made publicly available at https://github.com/DIaacKr/FedCoT</p></details> | EMNLP 2026 |

First, we introduce localization lens, a set of expert-validated representations that provide richer anatomical and positional context. However, as these representations increase input complexity, we integrate pixel shuffle within the model architecture to filter and refine representations, enhancing spatial information processing while preserving anatomical continuity. Lastly, to effectively align the localization lens representations with textual features, we incorporate decoupled contrastive loss (DCL) alongside the standard loss function. This ensures better feature discrimination and robustness, particularly in data limited medical settings. Through extensive evaluations on medical visual question answering (Med-VQA) datasets, we show that our methodology improves localization-driven performance across different Med-VLM architectures. Our analysis of localization-based questions further reveals that improvements in anatomy and spatial reasoning directly enhance the overall accuracy of Med-VQA upto 6.2%. | **[SAM Meets VLM: Parameter-Decoupled Full-Parameter Training for Unified Medical Reasoning and Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37283v1)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly expected not only to answer clinical questions, but also to localize the visual evidence behind their predictions. | **[STAR-GRPO: Canonical Anchoring and Reliability-First Advantages against Representation-Dependent Reward Hacking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36900v1)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reward hacking occurs when policy optimization exploits a brittle reward interface or an overly permissive proxy objective, improving the training score without improving the underlying response quality. This phenomenon is amplified in group-relative policy optimization: an unsupported reward can shift the group baseline and alter the updates of other rollouts, while post-hoc or purely relative weighting cannot represent group-wide uncertainty. We propose \emph{Self-Tuned Anchored Reliability Group-Relative Policy Optimization} (STAR-GRPO), a reliability-first advantage estimator based on paired assessments of the same rollout. STAR separates the quality signal from its learning influence: score disagreement determines rollout reliability, relative reliability enters a self-tuned robust location--scale fit before group normalization, and absolute group reliability attenuates the resulting bounded advantage. The analysis establishes coordinate and second-moment bounds, characterizes exact centering through the weighted location equation, and gives reliability-dependent attenuation guarantees for outlying rewards. We evaluate STAR-GRPO in two complementary reward-hacking regimes. Together, these results show that reliability-first normalization offers a principled way to limit unsupported reward influence on both group baselines and policy updates, while retaining the task reward as the optimization target.</p></details> |  |
| **[GenMem: Generative Symbolic Memory for Self-Evolving Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34633v1)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-term memory supports the self-evolution of LLM agents by retaining experience and skills across tasks and enabling their retrieval, reuse, and revision in subsequent long-horizon decision-making. Yet existing memory management approaches remain limited to discriminative retrieval and to address the sparse, hierarchical, and highly redundant structure of reusable experience: only a small, task-dependent subset of trajectories and memories warrants retention, retrieval, or revision. Learning these operations is further complicated by sparse, delayed, and indirect task-level feedback, with weak supervision across the memory lifecycle. | **[Breaking Failure Cascades: Step-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Medical Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31825v2)** | 2026-09-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent multimodal large language models have shown great promise in clinical image reasoning, but existing post-training pipelines remain predominantly outcome-centric, relying on final answer correctness or sequence-level preferences. | **[Not What You Meant: Can LLMs Follow a Specified Negation Semantics?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27517v1)** | 2026-09-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Negation does not carry a uniform interpretation across domains. Each program is solved under all four viewpoints using SWI-Prolog, a well-founded semantics solver, and clingo, yielding up to four divergent labels. The programs are then verbalized into natural language under multiple framings and rule orderings that leave the answer invariant. The results expose a consistent gap. | **[Counteraction-Aware Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation for General Capability Recovery with Domain Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27115v2)** | 2026-09-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Domain specialization can improve LLM behavior, but often weakens the general capabilities inherited from the original model. Through real-world case analyses in legal reasoning, medical reasoning, and hiring, we show how failures of epistemic humility, epistemic access, and resistance to epistemic injustice can produce consequential harms that standard measures of accuracy, fairness, and usability do not address on their own. | **[Med-OPD: Improving Medical Vision-Language Models via Evidence-Aware On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16303v1)** | 2026-07-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical Vision-Language Models (Med-VLMs) require reliable reasoning from fine-grained visual evidence, yet existing models can produce plausible clinical answers by relying on language priors or medical templates rather than truly attending to diagnosis-critical regions. However, standard OPD uniformly distills all tokens, causing sparse evidence-dependent tokens to be diluted by abundant clinical narrative tokens. Inspired by the success of OPD in the large language model community, we propose \textbf{Med-OPD}, to our knowledge the first unified post-training framework that integrates on-policy distillation with medical evidence-aware supervision for Med-VLMs. We introduce \textbf{Medical Evidence Advantage} (MEA), a teacher-grounded counterfactual signal that uses an answer-aware hint to focus teacher scoring on evidence supporting the target diagnosis, and measures each token's dependence on medical visual evidence by comparing teacher likelihoods under the original and evidence-degraded imaging modalities. Based on MEA, Med-OPD redistributes the distillation signal at both the token and trajectory levels, emphasizing diagnosis-critical tokens and evidence-reliant rollouts. Experiments on OmniMedVQA subsets show that Med-OPD consistently outperforms SFT and standard OPD across CT, MRI, Disease Diagnosis, and Lesion Grading. These results demonstrate that evidence-aware distillation can better strengthen medical VLMs' reliance on key visual evidence and improve reliable multimodal medical reasoning. The source code and data is publicly available at: https://github.com/yunhang8658/MedOPD.git</p></details> |  |
| **[Aligning Clinical Needs and AI Capabilities: A Survey on LLMs for Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07761v1)** | 2026-07-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as important tools in healthcare, showing growing potential for clinical reasoning and patient care. | **[Between Knowledge and Care: A Mixed-Methods Evaluation of Generative AI for T2DM Self-Management from Patient and Physician Perspectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03720v1)** | 2026-07-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative AI is increasingly used for everyday health guidance, yet its clinical appropriateness in chronic disease contexts remains poorly understood. Models perform well on factual explanation and lifestyle guidance but consistently underperform on medication reasoning and emotional support. Two \revise{analytic concepts} emerge \revise{from the data}. The \textit{pre-visit primer} \revise{frames AI as preparation for clinical encounters rather than as a replacement for physicians}. The \textit{fluency illusion} \revise{describes how polished language may convey epistemic authority that the clinical content does not support}. Patients and physicians converged on three shared limitations (role boundaries, emotional inadequacy, personalization gaps) while diverging in evaluative emphasis, \revise{which informed} four design directions, task-aware orchestration, risk-aware fallback, dynamic personalization, and emotionally attuned interaction.</p></details> | <details><summary>arXiv...</summary><p>arXiv admin note: text overlap with arXiv:2510.10048</p></details> |

| **[Evo-PI: Aligning Medical Reasoning via Evolving Principle-Guided Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31800v1)** | 2026-06-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite recent progress, the reasoning capabilities of large multimodal language models (MLLMs) remain fundamentally constrained by static supervision, where fixed prompts, rules, or reward models provide non-adaptive guidance throughout training. To address this gap, this paper develops a critical review at the intersection of philosophy of science and XAI. It examines prevailing accounts of what counts as an explanation in the health sciences and assesses their adequacy for informing XAI in medicine, arguing that they provide necessary conditions for a philosophically grounded approach to explainability in this domain. Building on this foundational philosophical literature, the discussion identifies three central axes of analysis: the role of causality in medical reasoning, the epistemic and relational dimensions of medical trust, and the criteria of explanatory adequacy as shaped by the pragmatic needs of diverse stakeholders. By integrating philosophical analysis with current developments in medical AI, the paper outlines principles for designing XAI systems that offer explanations that are not only epistemically robust but also aligned with the epistemic and practical requirements of clinical decision-making, shaping ongoing debates in medical XAI toward underexplored conceptual foundations.</p></details> |  |

Experiments on multiple medical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that PRA consistently outperforms strong baselines, achieving 81.9% accuracy on MedQA with Qwen3-4B, a new state of the art at the 4B scale. | **[MedCoG: Maximizing LLM Inference Density in Medical Reasoning via Meta-Cognitive Regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07905v2)** | 2026-05-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong potential in complex medical reasoning yet face diminishing gains under inference scaling laws. | **[When Cases Get Rare: A Retrieval Benchmark for Off-Guideline Clinical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21807v1)** | 2026-05-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Across medical specialties, clinical practice is anchored in evidence-based guidelines that codify best studied diagnostic and treatment pathways. Furthermore, UniR demonstrates weak-to-strong generalization, where reasoning modules trained on smaller models effectively guide much larger LLMs in the same model family, and generalize across domains such as in vision language models and medical reasoning.
